\varphi_{G}^{\beta^{\prime}}\eta=\alpha_{0}^{-\beta^{\prime}}(\varphi_{G}^{\beta^{\prime}}\circ U_{t_{0}}^{\beta^{\prime}})\eta=\alpha_{0}^{-\beta^{\prime}}\mathbb{I}_{\beta^{\prime}}\star(U_{t_{1}}^{\beta^{\prime}}\eta)=\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\alpha_{0}}\right)^{\beta^{\prime}}\mathbb{I}_{\beta^{\prime}}\star\eta.

Furthermore, one can easily see that
\operatorname{Ev}^{\dagger,\circ}_{\kappa,j,\chi,\beta}(\mathbb{I}_{\beta^{\prime}}\star-)=\operatorname{Ev}^{\dagger,\circ}_{\kappa,j,\chi,\beta}(-)
because
\mathbb{I}_{\beta^{\prime}}\cdot 1_{U_{G,\beta}^{\circ},\chi_{p}}=1_{U_{G,\beta}^{\circ},\chi_{p}}
. Theorem
8.5.1
now follows from Corollary
8.5.13
.

8.6.
Results from higher Coleman theory

In this section we recall the main results from
[
BP21
]
which we will use in this article. We begin by recalling the definition of “small slope”. Recall that
T^{G,-}\subset T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
denotes the submonoid of elements
t\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
which satisfy
v_{p}(\alpha(t))\leq 0
for any positive root
\alpha
of
G
. Let
T^{G,--}\subset T^{G,-}
denote the semigroup of elements
t\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
which satisfy
v_{p}(\alpha(t))<0
for any positive root
\alpha
of
G
.

Definition 8.6.1
.

Let
M
be a
L
-module (or a complex of
L
-modules in the derived category) equipped with an action of
T^{G,-}
, and suppose that there exists an element
x\in T^{G,--}
such that for all
h\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
, the module
M
has a slope
\leq h
decomposition with respect to the action of
x
(see
[
BP21
, §5.1]
). Let
\kappa\in X^{*}(T)
be a
M_{G}
-dominant character satisfying
C(\kappa^{*})^{-}=\{w_{n}\}
(see
[
BP21
, §5.10.1]
for the definition of
C(\kappa^{*})^{-}
; here
\kappa^{*}=-w_{M_{G}}^{\operatorname{max}}\kappa
).

(1)

We say a monoid homomorphism
\theta\colon T^{G,-}\to L^{\times}
satisfies the small slope condition
(-,\operatorname{ss}^{M}(\kappa^{*}))
if, for every
w\in{{}^{M}W_{G}}-\{w_{n}\}
, there exists an element
y\in T^{G,-}
such that

v_{p}(\theta(y))<v_{p}((w^{-1}\star\kappa^{*})(y))-v_{p}((w_{n}^{-1}\star\kappa^{*})(y))

where
w^{-1}\star\kappa^{*}=w^{-1}\cdot(\kappa^{*}+\rho_{G})-\rho_{G}
and
\rho_{G}
denotes the half-sum of positive roots of
G
.

(2)

We say a monoid homomorphism
\theta\colon T^{G,-}\to L^{\times}
satisfies the small slope condition
(-,\operatorname{ss}_{M,w_{n}}(\kappa^{*}))
if, for every
w\in W_{M_{G}}-\{1\}
, there exists an element
y\in T^{G,-}
such that

v_{p}(\theta(y))<v_{p}((w_{n}^{-1}w\star\kappa^{*})(y))-v_{p}((w_{n}^{-1}\star\kappa^{*})(y)).

(3)

Let
\lambda\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}-w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}(w_{n}^{-1}\star\kappa^{*})
. We say a monoid homomorphism
\theta\colon T^{G,-}\to L^{\times}
satisfies the small slope condition
(-,\operatorname{ss}(\lambda))
if, for every
w\in W_{G}
with
w\star\lambda\neq\lambda
, there exists an element
y\in T^{G,-}
such that

v_{p}(\theta(y))<v_{p}(-w\star(w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}\lambda)(y))-v_{p}(-w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}\lambda(y)).

(4)

For
\operatorname{ss}\in\{\operatorname{ss}^{M}(\kappa^{*}),\operatorname{ss}_{M_{w_{n}}}(\kappa^{*}),\operatorname{ss}(\lambda)\}
, we let
M^{(-,\operatorname{ss})}
denote the sum of generalised eigenspaces of
M^{\leq h}
with eigencharacter given by a
(-,\operatorname{ss})
small slope homomorphism, for
h
sufficiently large. This subspace is independent of
h\gg 0
(and also of the choice of
x
above).

Remark 8.6.2
.

Note that these small slope conditions are normalised differently from those in
[
BP21
, §5.11]
because we will always view
\theta
as an eigensystem for the optimally normalised
U_{p}
Hecke operators. Furthermore, as explained in
[
BP21
, Proposition 5.11.10]
, the small slope condition
(-,\operatorname{ss}(\lambda))
is satisfied if and only if both
(-,\operatorname{ss}^{M}(\kappa^{*}))
and
(-,\operatorname{ss}_{M,w_{n}}(\kappa^{*}))
are satisfied. The condition
(-,\operatorname{ss}(\lambda))
is the usual one appearing in the theory of
p
-adic families of automorphic forms via singular cohomology, and is the condition that we will impose in §
9
.

Example 8.6.3
.

Let
\kappa\in X^{*}(T)
be a
M_{G}
-dominant character such that
C(\kappa^{*})^{-}=\{w_{n}\}
. Let

M\in\left\{R\Gamma\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right),R\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta})}\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right),R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n}}(\kappa^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}\right\}.

The
M
carries an action of
T^{G,-}
via the action of the Hecke operators introduced in §
8.3
(i.e. the action of
t\in T^{G,-}
is through the action of the Hecke operator
U_{t}
). In this setting, we fix once and for all an element
t_{\operatorname{aux}}\in T^{G,--}
and consider all slope decompositions on
M
with respect to the action of
U_{t_{\operatorname{aux}}}
. Any small slope part we consider will be with respect to this action.

We would like to compare these cohomology groups for varying
\beta
. To do this we must introduce the relevant trace maps on these cohomology complexes.

Lemma 8.6.4
.

Let
\beta\geq 1
and suppose
L\supset\mu_{p^{\beta+1}}
. Let
\kappa\in X^{*}(T)
be a
M_{G}
-dominant character. Let

M_{\beta}\in\left\{R\Gamma\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right),R\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta})}\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right),R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n}}(\kappa^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}\right\}.

(1)

One has trace maps

\operatorname{Tr}\colon M_{\beta+1}\to M_{\beta}

which are equivariant for
T^{G,-}
(and the action of Hecke operators away from
p
).

(2)

One has a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n}}(\kappa^{*};\beta+1)^{(-,\dagger)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta+1})}\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta+1}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\Gamma\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta+1}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n}}(\kappa^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta})}\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\Gamma\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{res}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{cores}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{res}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{cores}}

(3)

One has a factorisation:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\beta+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\beta+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\beta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{x}}

for any
x\in T^{G,--}
.

Proof.

The trace maps are induced from the trace map associated with the forgetful map

f\colon\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta+1})\to\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})

as in
[
BP21
, Lemma 2.1.2]
, using the identification
f^{*}\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}=\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}
(note that
f(\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta+1}))\subset\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta})
). The equivariance follows from
[
BP21
, Lemma 4.2.14]
. Part (2) is clear from construction. Part (3) is also very similar to
[
BP21
, Lemma 4.2.14]
. For example, let

\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}}\xleftarrow{p_{1}}\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{p_{2}}\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}}

denote the correspondence associated with
x
as in §
8.2
(with
K_{p}=K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})
). Then
K_{p}^{\prime}\subset K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta+1})
and we have a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta+1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta+1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(because for any
m\geq 1
, we have
\mathcal{Q}_{m}K_{p}x^{-1}\subset\mathcal{Q}_{m}x^{-1}K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta+1})
– c.f. the proof of Lemma
8.2.4
) which implies that the trace map
R\Gamma_{p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta}))}(\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}^{\prime}},p_{1}^{*}\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}})\to R\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta})}(\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}},\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}})
factorises as

\displaystyle R\Gamma_{p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta}))}(\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}^{\prime}},p_{1}^{*}\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}})

\displaystyle\to R\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta+1})}(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta+1}),f^{*}\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}}R\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta})}(\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}},\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}).

∎

We have the following theorem.

Theorem 8.6.5

(Boxer–Pilloni)
.

Let
\kappa\in X^{*}(T)
be
M_{G}
-dominant with
C(\kappa^{*})^{-}=\{w_{n}\}
. Then for any
h\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
,
\beta\geq 1
, and
L\supset\mu_{p^{\beta}}
:

(1)

The cohomology complexes

R\Gamma\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right),R\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta})}\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right),R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n}}(\kappa^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}

admit slope
\leq h
decompositions.

(2)

The following morphisms

(8.6.6)

R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n}}(\kappa^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}\xleftarrow{\operatorname{res}}R\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p^{\beta})}\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{cores}}R\Gamma\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right)

are quasi-isomorphisms on
(-,\operatorname{ss}^{M}(\kappa^{*}))
small slope parts, and
R\Gamma\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right)^{(-,\operatorname{ss}^{M}(\kappa^{*}))}
is concentrated in degree
n-1
.

(3)

Suppose
L\supset\mu_{p^{\beta+1}}
. Then the trace maps in Lemma
8.6.4
(1) are quasi-isomorphisms on slope
\leq h
parts.

Proof.

We first explain the proofs of (1) and (2) for
\beta=1
. Consider the correspondence associated with
t_{\operatorname{aux}}
:

\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}}\xleftarrow{p_{1}}\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{p_{2}}\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}}

as in §
8.2
, with
K_{p}=K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)
. Set
T(-)=p_{2}p_{1}^{-1}(-)
and
T^{t}(-)=p_{1}p_{2}^{-1}(-)
, and consider the morphism
\phi\colon p_{2}^{*}\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\to p_{1}^{*}\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}
given by
\phi\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(-w_{n}^{-1}\rho+\rho)(t_{\operatorname{aux}})\phi_{t_{\operatorname{aux}},\kappa^{*}}
. With notation as in
[
Gra21
, §3]
, set

\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\pi_{\operatorname{HT},K_{p}}^{-1}\left(]Y_{w_{n}}^{G}[\right),\quad\quad\mathcal{Z}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\pi_{\operatorname{HT},K_{p}}^{-1}\left(\overline{]X_{w_{n}}^{G}[}\right).

Then, as explained in
[
BP21
, §3.5]
, the pair
(\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{1})
forms an open/closed support condition for the correspondence above in the sense of
[
BP
, Definition 6.1.3]
. For
k,m\geq 1
, set
\mathcal{U}_{k}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}T^{k-1}(\mathcal{U}_{1})
and
\mathcal{Z}_{m}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(T^{t})^{m-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})
. Then
\{\mathcal{U}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{\mathcal{Z}_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
form a system of support conditions as in Definition
4.0.1
(see Example
4.0.7
).

Following §
4
, set
R\Gamma(\mathcal{U}_{k},\mathcal{Z}_{m})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}R\Gamma_{\mathcal{U}_{k}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m}}(\mathcal{U}_{k},\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}})
and
R\Gamma(\mathcal{U}_{\bullet},\mathcal{Z}_{\bullet})=\varinjlim_{k}\varprojlim_{m}R\Gamma(\mathcal{U}_{k},\mathcal{Z}_{m})
. Then we obtain Hecke operators
T=U_{t_{\operatorname{aux}}}
on
R\Gamma(\mathcal{U}_{k},\mathcal{Z}_{m})
(associated with the above correspondence and
\phi
) which are all compatible with varying
k
and
m
. By
[
BP21
, Lemma 2.5.25]
, the operator
T
on
R\Gamma(\mathcal{U}_{k},\mathcal{Z}_{m})
is potent compact, hence one has slope decompositions for any
h\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
. Furthermore
R\Gamma(\mathcal{U}_{k},\mathcal{Z}_{m})^{\leq h}
is identified with
R\Gamma(\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{1})^{\leq h}
via the various restriction/corestriction maps (see
[
BP21
, §5.3]
for more details). Similarly,
R\Gamma(\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}},\mathcal{Z}_{1})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}R\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}(\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}},\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}})
and
R\Gamma(\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}R\Gamma(\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}},\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}})
admit slope
\leq h
decompositions for any
h\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.

As explained in the proof of
[
Gra21
, Theorem 4.6.6]
, the morphisms

R\Gamma(\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{1})\xleftarrow{\operatorname{res}}R\Gamma(\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}},\mathcal{Z}_{1})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{cores}}R\Gamma(\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}})

are quasi-isomorphisms on small slope parts
6
6

6

We have reversed the order of restriction and corestriction here (compared to the statement in
[
Gra21
, Theorem 4.6.6]
). However the vanishing results for the spectral sequence in
[
BP21
, §5]
imply this claim. More generally, there is a commutative square combining the maps here and in
[
Gra21
, Theorem 4.6.6]
, with all maps becoming quasi-isomorphisms on small slope parts.
, which implies that the following morphisms

(8.6.7)

R\Gamma(\mathcal{U}_{\bullet},\mathcal{Z}_{\bullet})\xleftarrow{\operatorname{res}}\varprojlim_{m}R\Gamma(\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}},\mathcal{Z}_{m})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{cores}}R\Gamma(\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}})

are also quasi-isomorphisms on small slope parts. Additionally, one can show that
R\Gamma(\mathcal{S}_{G,K_{p}})^{(-,\operatorname{ss}^{M}(\kappa^{*}))}
is concentrated in degree
n-1
. Now the proofs of (1) and (2) for
\beta=1
follow from the fact that
\cap_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{Z}_{m}=\mathcal{Z}_{G,>n+1}(p)
and the systems of support conditions

\{\mathcal{U}_{k}\},\{\mathcal{Z}_{m}\}\text{ and }\{\mathcal{U}^{G}_{K_{p},k}\},\{\overline{\pi_{\operatorname{HT},K_{p}^{-1}}(\mathcal{P}_{G}\backslash\mathcal{P}_{G}\mathcal{Q}_{m}K_{p})}\}

are intertwined in the sense of Definition
4.0.3
(which can be checked on the level of flag varieties). Hence the diagram (
8.6.7
) is identified with (
8.6.6
).

The general case now follows from Lemma
8.6.4
(crucially using the factorisation in part (3)).
∎

We now discuss the result for general
p
-adic weights. Let
(R,R^{+})
be a Tate affinoid pair over
(L,\mathcal{O}_{L})
and let
\kappa_{R}\colon T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to R^{\times}
be an
s_{0}
-analytic character, for some auxiliary integer
s_{0}\geq 1
. For any morphism
(R,R^{+})\to(S,S^{+})
of Tate affinoid pairs over
(L,\mathcal{O}_{L})
, we let
\kappa_{S}\colon T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to S^{\times}
denote the induced
s_{0}
-analytic character. If
L^{\prime}/L
is a finite extension and
z\colon(R,R^{+})\to(L^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}})
is a morphism over
(L,\mathcal{O}_{L})
, then we will also use the notation
\kappa_{z}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}z\circ\kappa_{R}
.

For any
s\geq s_{0}
, we have the cohomology complex
R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n},s\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{R}^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}
, and one can define a trace map

(8.6.8)

\operatorname{Tr}\colon R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n},s\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{R}^{*};\beta+1)^{(-,\dagger)}\to R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n},s\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{R}^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}

which is equivariant for
T^{G,-}
in a similar way to Lemma
8.6.4
.

Theorem 8.6.9

(Boxer–Pilloni)
.

Fix
L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
a finite extension containing
\mu_{p}
, and let
(R,R^{+})
be a Tate affinoid pair over
(L,\mathcal{O}_{L})
with
s_{0}
-analytic character
\kappa_{R}
as above. Then for any
z_{0}\colon(R,R^{+})\to(L,\mathcal{O}_{L})
and
h\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
, there exists an open neighbourhood
\operatorname{Spa}(S,S^{+})\subset\operatorname{Spa}(R,R^{+})
containing
z_{0}
such that: for all
\beta\geq 1
, for all finite extensions
L^{\prime}/L
containing
\mu_{p^{\beta}}
, and for all
s\geq s_{0}

(1)

The cohomology complex
R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n},s\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S^{\prime}}^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}
admits a slope
\leq h
decomposition, where
\operatorname{Spa}(S^{\prime},(S^{\prime})^{+})=\operatorname{Spa}(S,S^{+})\times_{\operatorname{Spa}(L,\mathcal{O}_{L})}\operatorname{Spa}(L^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}})
.

(2)

The natural map

R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n},(s+1)\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S^{\prime}}^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}\to R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n},s\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S^{\prime}}^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}

is a quasi-isomorphism on slope
\leq h
parts.

(3)

For any
z\in\operatorname{Spa}(S^{\prime},(S^{\prime})^{+})(L^{\prime})
such that
\kappa_{z}\in X^{*}(T)
is
M_{G}
-dominant and satisfies
C(\kappa_{z}^{*})^{-}=\{w_{n}\}
, the natural map (induced by the map from distributions to the algebraic representation)

R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n},s\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{z}^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}\to R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n}}(\kappa_{z}^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}

is a quasi-isomorphism on
(-,\operatorname{ss}_{M,w_{n}}(\kappa_{z}^{*}))
small slope parts.

(4)

Suppose
L^{\prime}
contains
\mu_{p^{\beta+1}}
. Then the trace map

\operatorname{Tr}\colon R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n},s\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S^{\prime}}^{*};\beta+1)^{(-,\dagger)}\to R\Gamma^{G}_{w_{n},s\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S^{\prime}}^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}

is a quasi-isomorphism on slope
\leq h
parts.

Proof.

As in Lemma
8.6.4
, one can establish an analogous factorisation diagram for
U_{t_{\operatorname{aux}}}
and the trace map in (
8.6.8
). Hence it suffices to prove the theorem when
\beta=1
. But part (1) just follows the same strategy as in the proof of Theorem
8.6.5
, by considering the system of support conditions
\{\mathcal{U}_{k}\}_{k\geq k_{0}}
,
\{\mathcal{Z}_{m}\}_{m\geq m_{0}}
for
k_{0},m_{0}
sufficiently large (because the locally projective Banach sheaf associated with
D_{G,\kappa_{S^{\prime}}^{*}}^{s\operatorname{-an}}
can be defined on an open neighbourhood of
p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U}_{k_{0}})\cap p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{m_{0}})
). Part (2) follows from the fact that the action of
t_{\operatorname{aux}}
on
D_{G,\kappa_{S^{\prime}}^{*}}^{s\operatorname{-an}}
factors through
D_{G,\kappa_{S^{\prime}}^{*}}^{(s+1)\operatorname{-an}}\to D_{G,\kappa_{S^{\prime}}^{*}}^{s\operatorname{-an}}
(c.f.
[
BP21
, §6.4.4]
). Part (3) follows from analysing the locally analytic BGG resolution (see
[
BP21
, Corollary 6.8.4]
– the expected slope bounds hold because the Shimura variety is compact).
∎

8.7.
Trace-compatibility of overconvergent evaluation maps

The final ingredient we need for constructing the
p
-adic
L
-function is a trace-compatibility relation between
\operatorname{Ev}^{\dagger,\circ}_{\kappa,j,\chi,\beta}
for varying
\beta
.

Lemma 8.7.1
.

Let
(\kappa,j)\in\mathcal{E}
satisfying Assumption
5.3.12
and let
\beta\geq 1
be an integer. Let
\chi\in\Sigma_{\kappa,j}(\mathfrak{N}_{\beta})
such that the conductor of
\chi
is prime to
p
(i.e. divides
\mathfrak{N}
). Let
L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
be a finite extension containing
F^{\operatorname{cl}}(\chi)
and
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p^{\beta+1}})
. Then

\operatorname{Ev}^{\dagger,\circ}_{\kappa,j,\chi,\beta}\circ\operatorname{Tr}=\operatorname{Ev}^{\dagger,\circ}_{\kappa,j,\chi,\beta+1}

where
\operatorname{Tr}\colon\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n}}(\kappa^{*};\beta+1)^{(-,\dagger)}\to\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n}}(\kappa^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}
is the trace map as in Lemma
8.6.4
.

Proof.

Note that
\operatorname{Ev}^{\dagger,\circ}_{\kappa,j,\chi,\beta}=\operatorname{Ev}^{\dagger}_{\kappa,j,\chi,\beta}\circ(1_{U_{G,\beta}^{\circ}}\star-)
and
\operatorname{Ev}^{\dagger}_{\kappa,j,\chi,\beta}\circ\operatorname{Tr}=\operatorname{Ev}^{\dagger}_{\kappa,j,\chi,\beta+1}
for the same reasons as in Proposition
5.3.10
. Therefore, it suffices to show that

(1_{U_{G,\beta}^{\circ}}\star-)\circ\operatorname{Tr}=\operatorname{Tr}\circ(1_{U_{G,\beta+1}^{\circ}}\star-).

As in §
8.5
, we can reduce this to a statement over the ordinary locus, and the claim follows from the fact that
\operatorname{Tr}
is expressed as the sum over the action of representatives of
M^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})/M^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta+1})
and
1_{U_{G,\beta}^{\circ}}
is fixed under the action of
M^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})
.
∎

8.8.
Test data for the
p
-adic
L
-function

We now describe the general recipe for constructing the
p
-adic
L
-function, which we will be used in §
9
. Fix
L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
a finite extension containing
\mu_{p}
and
\iota_{p}(F_{\mathfrak{N}})
. Suppose that
(R,R^{+})
is a Tate affinoid pair over
(L,\mathcal{O}_{L})
, and we have a
s_{0}
-analytic character
\kappa_{R}\colon T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to R^{\times}
satisfying Assumption
5.3.12
. Let
z_{0}\in\operatorname{Spa}(R,R^{+})(L)
such that
\kappa_{z_{0}}\in X^{*}(T)
satisfies the conditions in Definition
2.1.3
and
C(\kappa_{z_{0}}^{*})^{-}=\{w_{n}\}
. Let
\lambda_{R}=-w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}(w_{n}^{-1}\star\kappa_{R}^{*})
. Fix a rational number
h\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.

Let
\Omega=\operatorname{Spa}(S,S^{+})
be the neighbourhood of
z_{0}
as in Theorem
8.6.9
. We let
\Upsilon\subset\Omega(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
denote a subset of points
z\in\Omega(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
such that:

•

z_{0}\in\Upsilon
;

•

for any
z\in\Upsilon
, the character
\kappa_{z}\in X^{*}(T)
satisfies the conditions in Definition
2.1.3
and
C(\kappa_{z}^{*})^{-}=\{w_{n}\}
.

Recall the definition of
\Omega_{s}=\Omega\times_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{N}p^{\infty})_{s}
from §
7.4.4
. Let
\Sigma^{\prime}_{\beta}\subset\bigcup_{s\geq 1}\Omega_{s}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
denote the maximal subset of points
x\in\bigcup_{s\geq 1}\Omega_{s}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
such that its projection to
\Omega
lies in
\Upsilon
, and which satisfy the conditions in Notation
7.4.19
. Let
\Sigma\subset\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}
denote the largest subset such that
\chi_{x}
has conductor dividing
\mathfrak{N}
for any
x\in\Sigma
. Note that

\Sigma\subset\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}\subset\Sigma_{2}^{\prime}\subset\cdots.

We now introduce the test data.

Definition 8.8.1
.

With set-up as above, let
\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}\subset\Upsilon
be a subset containing
z_{0}
and let

\eta\in\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}

denote a cohomology class such that:

•

for any
z\in\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}
, the specialisation
\eta_{z,1,s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}\in\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{z}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}
of
\eta
at the point
z
is an eigenvector for the action of
T^{G,-}
with eigencharacter
\theta_{z}
satisfying the
(-,\operatorname{ss}(\lambda_{z}))
small slope condition.

Definition 8.8.2
.

Let
\eta
be as in Definition
8.8.1
, and for
s\geq s_{0}
, let
\eta_{1,s\operatorname{-an}}\in\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n},s\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}
denote the unique lift of
\eta
under the isomorphism in Theorem
8.6.9
(2). Suppose that
\Sigma
is Zariski dense in
\Omega_{1}
. We define:

\Xi(\eta)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Ev}^{\dagger,\operatorname{la}}_{\kappa_{S},1}((\eta_{1,s-\operatorname{an}})_{s\geq s_{0}})\in\mathscr{D}^{\operatorname{la}}\left(\Gal(F_{\mathfrak{N}p^{\infty}}/F),\mathscr{O}_{\Omega}\right)

(equivalently, we can view
\Xi(\eta)
as a global section on
\Omega\times_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{N}p^{\infty})
).

We have the following interpolation property.

Theorem 8.8.3
.

With set-up as above, let
\eta
and
\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}
be as in Definition
8.8.1
. Suppose that
\Sigma
is Zariski dense in
\Omega_{1}
, and let

\Sigma^{\operatorname{int}}=\left\{x\in\bigcup_{\beta\geq 1}\Sigma_{\beta}^{\prime}:\begin{array}[]{c}\mathfrak{p}_{\tau_{0}}\text{ divides the conductor of }\chi_{x}\text{ and }\\
x\in\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}\times\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{N}p^{\infty})\end{array}\right\}.

Then, for any
x\in\Sigma^{\operatorname{int}}
, one has

\Xi(\eta)(x)=(1-p^{-1})A(x)\cdot\operatorname{Ev}_{x}(\eta_{z}^{\operatorname{cl}})

where

•

z\in\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}
denotes the projection of
x
to
\Omega
,
p^{\beta^{\prime}}
denotes the conductor of
\chi_{x,p,\bar{\tau}_{0}}
, and

A(x)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left(\frac{\theta_{z}(t_{0})}{\theta_{z}(t_{1})}\right)^{\beta^{\prime}}p^{\beta^{\prime}\kappa_{x,n+1,\tau_{0}}}\chi_{x}(\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}_{0}}})^{-\beta^{\prime}}\chi_{x,p,\bar{\tau}_{0}}(-1)\mathscr{G}(\chi_{x,p,\bar{\tau}_{0}})

with
\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}_{0}}}\in\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\times}
denoting the idele obtained as the image of
p
under the natural embedding
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\cong F_{\bar{\mathfrak{p}}_{\tau_{0}}}^{\times}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\times}
.

•

\operatorname{Ev}_{x}(\eta_{z}^{\operatorname{cl}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Ev}_{\kappa_{x},j_{x}-\chi_{x,p},\chi_{x},\beta}(\eta_{z,\beta}^{\operatorname{cl}})
where
\beta\geq 1
is any integer such that
x\in\Sigma_{\beta}^{\prime}
, and
\eta_{z,\beta}^{\operatorname{cl}}\in\operatorname{H}^{n-1}\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa_{z}^{*}}\right)^{(-,\operatorname{ss}(\lambda_{z}))}
denotes the unique class whose trace down to
\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p)
uniquely corresponds to
\eta_{z,1,s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}
via the isomorphisms in Theorem
8.6.9
(3) and Theorem
8.6.5
(2).

Proof.

Note that
\operatorname{Ev}_{x}(\eta_{z}^{\operatorname{cl}})
is indeed independent of
\beta
by Proposition
5.3.15
. We will define an analogue of
\Xi(\eta)
for general
\beta\geq 1
. Let
L_{\beta}=L(\mu_{p^{\beta}})
, and set
\operatorname{Spa}(S_{\beta},S_{\beta}^{+})=\Omega_{L_{\beta}}
. Let
\eta_{\beta,s\operatorname{-an}}\in\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n},s\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S_{\beta}}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}
denote the unique lift of
\eta
under the morphisms in Theorem
8.6.9
. Then we define
\Xi_{\beta}(\eta)
as

\Xi_{\beta}(\eta)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Ev}^{\dagger,\operatorname{la}}_{\kappa_{S_{\beta}},\beta}\left((\eta_{\beta,s\operatorname{-an}})_{s\geq\operatorname{max}(s_{0},\beta)}\right)\in\mathscr{D}^{\operatorname{la}}\left(\Gal(F_{\mathfrak{N}p^{\infty}}/F),\mathscr{O}_{\Omega_{L_{\beta}}}\right)

We see from §
7.4.5
and Theorem
8.5.1
that, for any
x\in\Sigma^{\operatorname{int}}\cap\Sigma_{\beta}^{\prime}
, one has the interpolation property:

\Xi_{\beta}(\eta)(x)=(1-p^{-1})A(x)\cdot\operatorname{Ev}_{x}(\eta_{z}^{\operatorname{cl}}).

Note that, by tracing through the definitions and using Proposition
2.3.6
,
\operatorname{Ev}_{\kappa_{x},j_{x}-\chi_{x,p},\chi_{x},\beta}^{\dagger}
does indeed correspond to
\operatorname{Ev}_{\kappa_{x},j_{x}-\chi_{x,p},\chi_{x},\beta}
via the classicality isomorphisms in Theorem
8.6.5
(2).

In addition to this, for any
x\in\Sigma
we have, by Lemma
8.7.1
, the compatibility

\Xi_{\beta}(\eta)(x)=\operatorname{Ev}^{\dagger,\circ}_{\kappa_{x},j_{x},\chi_{x},\beta}(\operatorname{sp}_{x}(\eta_{\beta,\operatorname{la}}))=\operatorname{Ev}^{\dagger,\circ}_{\kappa_{x},j_{x},\chi_{x},1}(\operatorname{sp}_{x}(\eta_{1,\operatorname{la}}))=\Xi(\eta)(x)

where
\operatorname{sp}_{x}(\eta_{\beta,\operatorname{la}})\in\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n}}(\kappa_{x}^{*};\beta)^{(-,\dagger)}
denotes the specialisation of
\eta_{\beta,s\operatorname{-an}}
(for any
s\geq\operatorname{max}(s_{0},\beta)
) at the point
x\in\Sigma
(note that
\operatorname{sp}_{x}(\eta_{\beta,\operatorname{la}})
are trace-compatible for varying
\beta
). Since
\Sigma
is Zariski dense, this implies that the image of
\Xi(\eta)
in
\mathscr{D}^{\operatorname{la}}\left(\Gal(F_{\mathfrak{N}p^{\infty}}/F),\mathscr{O}_{\Omega_{L_{\beta}}}\right)
must coincide with
\Xi_{\beta}(\eta)
. The result follows.
∎

9.
Automorphic representations

In this section we introduce the relevant automorphic representations of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
and construct the
p
-adic
L
-functions in Theorem
A
and Theorem
B
.

9.1.
Assumptions

Let
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
. Write
\pi=\otimes_{v}^{\prime}\pi_{v}
for its restricted tensor product decomposition over the places of
\mathbb{Q}
. By abuse of notation, we will also use the notation
\pi_{\infty}
to denote the underlying
(\mathfrak{g},K_{\infty})
-module, where
K_{\infty}\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{R})
is the maximal compact-mod-centre subgroup whose complexification equals
M_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{C})
. We impose the following hypotheses:

•

(“generic at
\infty
”) We suppose that
\pi_{\infty}
lies in the discrete series with Harish-Chandra parameter of the form
w_{n}\cdot(\lambda+\rho_{G})
for some dominant
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)^{+}
. In particular, we set
\kappa\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}-w_{M_{G}}^{\operatorname{max}}\cdot(w_{n}\star(-w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}\lambda))
, which satisfies
C(\kappa^{*})^{-}=\{w_{n}\}
.

•

(“self-duality”) The character
\lambda
is self-dual, i.e.,
\lambda=-w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}\lambda
.

•

Let
S
be a finite set of places of
\mathbb{Q}
containing
\infty
and all primes where
\pi
is ramified.
7
7

7

We say
\pi
ramifies at
\ell
if there does not exist a maximal special subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
under which
\pi_{\ell}
has non-trivial invariants.
We let
K\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
be a neat compact open subgroup such that
\pi_{f}^{K}\neq 0
. We suppose that
K
factorises as

K=K_{S}K^{S},\quad\quad K_{S}\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f,S}),\;K^{S}\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}^{S}),

where
K^{S}=\prod_{\ell\not\in S}K_{\ell}
with
K_{\ell}\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
a good maximal special compact open subgroup (as in
[
Min11
, §2.1]
). We assume that
p\not\in S
and that
K_{p}
is hyperspecial (and identifies with
G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
with respect to a fixed reductive integral model of
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
).

We let
\mathfrak{N}
denote the smallest ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
such that

\nu(K^{p}\cap\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p}))\subset\mathcal{N}((\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{F^{+}}^{(p)}+\mathfrak{N}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{(p)})^{\times})

(see Definition
5.3.11
).

Remark 9.1.1
.

Write
\lambda=(\lambda_{0};\lambda_{1,\tau},\dots,\lambda_{2n,\tau})
and
\kappa=(\kappa_{0};\kappa_{1,\tau},\dots,\kappa_{2n,\tau})
. Since
\lambda
is self-dual, we have
\lambda_{0}=0
and
\lambda_{i,\tau}=-\lambda_{2n+1-i,\tau}
for any
i\in\{1,\dots,2n\}
and
\tau\in\Psi
. An explicit calculation then shows that
\kappa_{0}=0
and

\displaystyle(\kappa_{1,\tau_{0}},\dots,\kappa_{2n,\tau})

\displaystyle=(n-\lambda_{n+1,\tau_{0}},-\lambda_{2n,\tau_{0}},\dots,-\lambda_{n+2,\tau_{0}},-(1+\lambda_{n,\tau_{0}}),\dots,-(1+\lambda_{1,\tau_{0}}))

\displaystyle(\kappa_{1,\tau},\dots,\kappa_{2n,\tau})

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{1,\tau},\dots,\lambda_{2n,\tau})\quad\quad(\tau\neq\tau_{0}).

We therefore see that
\kappa
satisfies the assumptions in Definition
2.1.3
and also Assumption
5.3.12
.

9.1.1.
Test data

We consider the following vectors:

•

Let
\phi_{\infty}\in\pi_{\infty}
be a non-zero element in the lowest
K_{\infty}
-type which is an eigenvector for the action of
K_{\infty}\cap\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{R})
with eigencharacter
\sigma_{\kappa}^{[0],\vee}
(note the lowest
K_{\infty}
-type has highest weight
\kappa-2\rho_{H,\operatorname{nc}}
, so there does indeed exist such an eigenvector).

•

We fix a non-zero element
\phi_{S}\in\pi_{f,S}^{K_{S}}

•

For
\ell\notin S^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}S\cup\{p\}
, we fix a non-zero element
\phi_{\ell}\in\pi_{\ell}^{K_{\ell}}
. We let

\theta^{S^{\prime}}_{\pi}\colon C^{\infty}(K^{S^{\prime}}\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}^{S^{\prime}})/K^{S^{\prime}};\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{C}

denote the corresponding Hecke eigensystem with respect to a fixed Haar measure on
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}^{S^{\prime}})
, i.e., for any
f\in C^{\infty}(K^{S^{\prime}}\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}^{S^{\prime}})/K^{S^{\prime}};\mathbb{C})
, we have

f\cdot\phi^{S^{\prime}}=\theta_{\pi}^{S^{\prime}}(f)\phi^{S^{\prime}},\quad\quad\quad\phi^{S^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigotimes_{\ell\not\in S^{\prime}}\phi_{\ell}\in\bigotimes^{\prime}_{\ell\not\in S^{\prime}}\pi_{\ell}.

•

We suppose that there exists a monoid homomorphism
\theta_{\pi,p}\colon T^{G,-}\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
satisfying
(-,\operatorname{ss}(\lambda))
and a non-zero vector
\phi_{p}\in\pi_{p}^{K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)}
such that

\lambda(t^{\prime})[K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)\cdot t^{\prime}\cdot K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)]\cdot\phi_{p}=\theta_{\pi,p}(t)\phi_{p},\quad\quad t^{\prime}=(w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}})^{-1}tw_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}

for all
t\in T^{G,-}
. Here
[K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)\cdot t^{\prime}\cdot K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)]
denotes the (unnormalised) Hecke operator associated with the element
t^{\prime}
, and we are using the identification
\iota_{p}\colon\mathbb{C}\cong\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

We let
\phi\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\phi_{\infty}\otimes\phi_{S}\otimes\phi^{S^{\prime}}\otimes\phi_{p}
. Set

\mathbb{T}^{-}=C^{\infty}(K^{S^{\prime}}\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}^{S^{\prime}})/K^{S^{\prime}};\mathbb{Q})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}[T^{G,-}]

and let
\theta_{\pi}\colon\mathbb{T}^{-}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
denote the homomorphism given by
\theta_{\pi}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\theta^{S^{\prime}}_{\pi}\otimes\theta_{\pi,p}
. We note that
\theta_{\pi}
is defined over a number field (see
[
ST14
, Proposition 2.15]
), so there exists a finite extension
L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
such that
\theta_{\pi}
is defined over
L
(again using the fixed identification
\iota_{p}\colon\mathbb{C}\cong\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
). We assume that
L
contains
\mu_{p}
and
\iota_{p}(F_{\mathfrak{N}})
.

Definition 9.1.2
.

Let
u_{\operatorname{sph}}\in\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})=\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\times\prod_{\tau\in\Psi}\operatorname{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
denote the element
u_{\operatorname{sph}}=1\times\prod_{\tau}u_{\operatorname{sph},\tau}
where
u_{\operatorname{sph},\tau}
is the block matrix (with block size
n\times n
) given by

u_{\operatorname{sph},\tau}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&-w_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}^{\operatorname{max}}\\
&1\end{array}\right)

where
w_{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}^{\operatorname{max}}
denotes the antidiagonal matrix with
1
s along the antidiagonal. For tuples
j=(j_{\tau})\in\prod_{\tau\in\Psi}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
e=(e_{\tau})\in\prod_{\tau\in\Psi}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that
(\kappa,j)\in\mathcal{E}
, we define

\phi_{e}^{[j]}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left(\Delta_{\kappa}^{[j]}\cdot\phi_{\infty}\right)\otimes\phi_{S}\otimes\phi^{S^{\prime}}\otimes\left(u_{\operatorname{sph}}t_{p}^{e}\cdot\phi_{p}\right)\;\;\in\;\;\pi

where
\Delta_{\kappa}^{[j]}
is as in Definition
A.1.5
and
t_{p}^{e}\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
denotes the element

t_{p}^{e}=1\times\prod_{\tau}\operatorname{diag}(p^{e_{\tau}(2n-1)},p^{e_{\tau}(2n-2)},\dots,p^{e_{\tau}},1).

Remark 9.1.3
.

The automorphic forms in Definition
9.1.2
will be the input into the unitary Friedberg–Jacquet periods, and their definition is very closely related to the input data for the Friedberg–Jacquet periods for general linear groups (see
[
BDG+22
,
BGW23
]
). In fact, for the automorphic periods we consider to be non-trivial we should impose the following additional assumptions:

•

(“symplectic type”) The weak base-change of
\pi
to an automorphic representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{1}(\mathbb{A}_{E})\times\operatorname{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
is of symplectic type with trivial similitude character (see
[
CG21
, Conjecture 7.4(2)]
). Furthermore,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f})}(\pi_{f},\mathbb{C})\neq 0
.

•

\pi_{p}
is generic (i.e. isomorphic to an unramified principal series representation) with distinct Satake parameters in each
\tau
-component, and the choice of
p
-stabilisation
\phi_{p}
above is “spin” (see
[
BDG+22
, §6]
or
[
BGW23
, §3]
) .

In the constructions below, we prefer not to make these additional assumptions because this affords us with extra flexibility when deforming
\pi
in a Coleman family (and it is not necessary to assume
[
CG21
, Conjecture 7.4]
).

For the test data for the
p
-adic
L
-function however, we need to consider “dual versions” of these automorphic forms, which we introduce in the following section.

9.1.2.
Dual test data

Let
\beta\geq 1
be an integer, and set

s_{p}=1\times\prod_{\tau\in\Psi}\operatorname{diag}(1,p,p^{2},\dots,p^{2n-1})\;\in\;T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

View
w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
as the matrix which in the
\tau
-component is given by the the antidiagonal matrix with
1
s along the antidiagonal (for any
\tau\in\Psi
). We define:

\psi_{p,\beta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}\lambda)(s_{p})^{\beta}\theta_{\pi,p}(s_{p})^{-\beta}[K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})s_{p}^{\beta}w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)]\cdot\phi_{p}\;\in\;\pi_{p}^{K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})}.

Lemma 9.1.4
.

The class
\psi_{p,\beta}
satisfies

(9.1.5)

(w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}\lambda)(t)[K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})tK^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})]\cdot\psi_{p,\beta}=\theta_{\pi,p}(t)\psi_{p,\beta}

for any
t\in T^{G,-}
. Moreover
\operatorname{tr}(\psi_{p,\beta+1})=\psi_{p,\beta}
, where
\operatorname{tr}
denotes the (unnormalised) trace from level
K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta+1})
to level
K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})
.

Proof.

Let
t\in T^{G,-}
. Then an explicit calculation shows that

[K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})tK^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})]\circ[K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})s_{p}^{\beta}w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)]=[K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})s_{p}^{\beta}w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)]\circ[K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)t^{\prime}K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)]

where
t^{\prime}=(w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}})^{-1}tw_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}
. Indeed, set
y=s_{p}^{\beta}w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}
. Then both

(t^{-1}K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})t\cap K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}))\backslash K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})/(yK^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)y^{-1}\cap K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}))

and

(y^{-1}K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})y\cap K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p))\backslash K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)/(t^{\prime}K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)(t^{\prime})^{-1}\cap K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p))

are singletons (which can easily be checked using Iwahori decompositions). This implies the transformation property in (
9.1.5
). Furthermore, a similar calculation shows that

\operatorname{tr}\circ[K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta+1})s_{p}^{\beta+1}w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)]=[K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})s_{p}K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})]\circ[K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})s_{p}^{\beta}w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)]

which implies the trace compatibility.
∎

For an integer
\beta\geq 1
and a tuple
j=(j_{\tau})\in\prod_{\tau\in\Psi}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
(\kappa,j)\in\mathcal{E}
, we set

\psi_{\beta}^{[j]}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left(\Delta_{\kappa}^{[j]}\cdot\phi_{\infty}\right)\otimes\phi_{S}\otimes\phi^{S^{\prime}}\otimes\hat{\gamma}\cdot\psi_{p,\beta}\;\in\;\pi.

We now consider the corresponding cohomology classes. Let
V_{\kappa-2\rho_{H,\operatorname{nc}}}
denote the algebraic representation of
K_{\infty}
with highest weight
\kappa-2\rho_{H,\operatorname{nc}}
. Then there exists a unique
K_{\infty}
-equivariant homomorphism
F_{\infty}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}(V_{\kappa-2\rho_{H,\operatorname{nc}}},\pi_{\infty})
such that
F_{\infty}(\alpha_{-}\odot v_{\kappa}^{[0]})=\phi_{\infty}
, where
\alpha_{-}\in V_{-2\rho_{H,\operatorname{nc}}}=\bigwedge^{n-1}\overline{\mathfrak{u}}_{G}
denotes a generator of the line
\bigwedge^{n-1}\overline{\mathfrak{u}}_{H}
and
\odot
denotes the Cartan product. This extends to a
K_{\infty}
-equivariant homomorphism
F_{\infty}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}(\bigwedge^{n-1}\overline{\mathfrak{u}}_{G},\pi_{\infty}\otimes V_{\kappa}^{*})
in the obvious way. By Arthur’s multiplicity formula for unitary groups
[
CZ21
]
, the representation
\pi
appears with multiplicity one in the
K_{\circ}
-finite vectors
C^{\infty}([\mathbf{G}])^{K_{\circ}\operatorname{-fin}}
of the smooth functions on
[\mathbf{G}]=\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{Q})A_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
(see §
5.3.3
). We fix such an embedding and consider the induced homomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}(\bigwedge^{n-1}\overline{\mathfrak{u}}_{G},\pi_{\infty}\otimes V_{\kappa}^{*})\otimes\pi_{f}^{K^{p}K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}(\bigwedge^{n-1}\overline{\mathfrak{u}}_{G},C^{\infty}([\mathbf{G}]/K^{p}K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}))^{K_{\circ}\operatorname{-fin}}\otimes V_{\kappa}^{*}).

We let
\eta^{\operatorname{cl}}_{\pi,\beta}
denote the image of
F_{\infty}\otimes(\phi_{S}\otimes\phi^{S^{\prime}}\otimes\psi_{p,\beta})
under this embedding. Since the
(\overline{\mathfrak{p}}_{G},K_{\infty})
-cohomology of
\pi_{\infty}\otimes V_{\kappa}^{*}
is one-dimensional and concentrated in degree
n-1
(see
[
BHR94
, Theorem 3.2.1]
), this class
\eta^{\operatorname{cl}}_{\pi,\beta}
must represent a cohomology class in
\operatorname{H}^{n-1}\left(S_{\mathbf{G},\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})(\mathbb{C}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right)
which we also denote by
\eta^{\operatorname{cl}}_{\pi,\beta}
. By Lemma
9.1.4
, the classes
\eta^{\operatorname{cl}}_{\pi,\beta}
are trace-compatible as
\beta
varies, and are Hecke eigenvectors – for any
f\in C^{\infty}(K^{S^{\prime}}\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}^{S^{\prime}})/K^{S^{\prime}})
and
t\in T^{G,-}
we have

f\cdot\eta^{\operatorname{cl}}_{\pi,\beta}=\theta^{S^{\prime}}_{\pi}(f)\eta^{\operatorname{cl}}_{\pi,\beta},\quad\quad U_{t}\cdot\eta^{\operatorname{cl}}_{\pi,\beta}=\theta_{\pi,p}(t)\eta^{\operatorname{cl}}_{\pi,\beta}.

We can (and do) rescale
\phi=\phi_{\infty}\otimes\phi_{S}\otimes\phi^{S^{\prime}}\otimes\phi_{p}
so that
\eta^{\operatorname{cl}}_{\pi,1}
is defined over a number field, and hence by rigid GAGA, gives rise to a cohomology class in
\operatorname{H}^{n-1}\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right)
, where we view
\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})
as an adic space over
\operatorname{Spa}(L,\mathcal{O}_{L})
. This cohomology class lies in the
(-,\operatorname{ss}(\lambda))
part by assumption.

9.2.
Variation in the anticyclotomic direction

We now construct the
p
-adic
L
-function appearing in Theorem
A
. We first introduce the interpolation set.

Definition 9.2.1
.

Let
\Sigma_{\pi}
denote the set of anticyclotomic algebraic Hecke characters

\chi\colon F^{\times}\mathbb{A}_{F^{+}}^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}

such that:

•

The
\infty
-type of
\chi
is of the form

\chi(z)=z_{\tau_{0}}^{-(\lambda_{n,\tau_{0}}+1+j_{\tau_{0}})}\bar{z}_{\tau_{0}}^{\lambda_{n,\tau_{0}}+1+j_{\tau_{0}}}\cdot\prod_{\tau\in\Psi-\{\tau_{0}\}}z_{\tau}^{-j_{\tau}}\bar{z}_{\tau}^{j_{\tau}}

for all
z=(z_{\tau})\in(\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}F)^{\times}=\prod_{\tau\in\Psi}\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, for some tuple of integers
j=(j_{\tau})\in\prod_{\tau\in\Psi}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
j_{\tau_{0}}\leq\lambda_{n-1,\tau_{0}}-\lambda_{n,\tau_{0}}
and
j_{\tau}\leq\lambda_{n,\tau}
for
\tau\neq\tau_{0}
.

•

The conductor of
\chi
divides
\mathfrak{N}p^{\infty}
. In particular, we let
c=c(\chi)=(c_{\tau})\in\prod_{\tau\in\Psi}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
denote the tuple of integers such that the
p
-part of the conductor of
\chi
is of the form
\prod_{\tau\in\Psi}(\mathfrak{p}_{\tau}\cdot\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}})^{c_{\tau}}
.

•

One has
c_{\tau_{0}}\geq 1
.

For any such character satisfying these assumptions, we let
j=j(\chi)
denote the tuple in the first bullet point, and we let
e=e(\chi)=(e_{\tau})\in\prod_{\tau\in\Psi}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
denote the tuple satisfying
e_{\tau}=\operatorname{max}(c_{\tau},1)
.

For any
i=1,\dots,2n
and
\tau\in\Psi
, let
t_{p,i,\tau}\in\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
denote the element which is the identity outside the
\tau
-component, and in the
\tau
-component is given by
\operatorname{diag}(p,\dots,p,1,\dots,1)
where there are
i
lots of
p
. Let
\alpha_{i,\tau}\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
and
\alpha_{p}^{e}
denote the elements which satisfy

[K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)\cdot t_{p,i,\tau}\cdot K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)]\cdot\phi_{p}=\alpha_{i,\tau}\phi_{p}\quad\text{ and }\quad[K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)\cdot t_{p}^{e}\cdot K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)]\cdot\phi_{p}=\alpha_{p}^{e}\phi_{p}.

For any
\chi\in\Sigma_{\pi}
, we let
\mathscr{E}_{p}(\pi,\chi)
denote the following
p
-adic multiplier

\mathscr{E}_{p}(\pi,\chi)=p^{-e_{\tau_{0}}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{n,\tau_{0}}}{\alpha_{n-1,\tau_{0}}}\right)^{e_{\tau_{0}}}\chi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}_{0}}}(\operatorname{sgn}(w_{\operatorname{GL}_{n-1}}^{\operatorname{max}}))\chi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}_{0}}}(p)^{-e_{\tau_{0}}}\mathscr{G}(\chi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}_{0}}})\left(\prod_{\tau\neq\tau_{0}}\chi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}}}(-1)^{n}\right)(\alpha_{p}^{e}\delta_{B}(t_{p}^{e}))^{-1}

where
\delta_{B}
denotes the modulus function associated with the upper-triangular Borel subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, and
\chi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}}}
denotes the restriction of
\chi
to
F_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}}}^{\times}\cong\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
.

Theorem 9.2.2
.

Let
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation satisfying the assumptions in §
9.1
(except Remark
9.1.3
). Given test data
\phi
as in §
9.1
, there exists a locally analytic distribution
\mathscr{L}_{p,\phi}(\pi,-)\in\mathscr{D}^{\operatorname{la}}(\Gal(F_{\mathfrak{N}p^{\infty}}/F),L)
such that

\mathscr{L}_{p,\phi}(\pi,\hat{\chi})=(\star)\cdot(2\pi i)^{-(n-1)}\cdot\mathscr{E}_{p}(\pi,\chi)\cdot\int_{[\mathbf{H}]}\phi^{[j]}_{e}(h)\chi^{\prime}\left(\frac{\operatorname{det}h_{2}}{\operatorname{det}h_{1}}\right)dh

for any
\chi\in\Sigma_{\pi}
, where
(\star)
is a non-zero rational number independent of
\pi
and
\chi
.

Proof.

We wish to apply the general construction in §
8.8
. Let
\mathcal{W}_{0}
denote the (
n[F^{+}:\mathbb{Q}]
-dimensional) weight space over
\operatorname{Spa}(L,\mathcal{O}_{L})
parameterising continuous characters
\xi
on
T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
which satisfy
\xi=-w_{G}^{\operatorname{max}}\xi
. The weight
\lambda
therefore corresponds to a point
z_{0}\in\mathcal{W}_{0}(L)
. Let
\operatorname{Spa}(R,R^{+})\subset\mathcal{W}_{0}
be an open affinoid neighbourhood of
z_{0}
with
s_{0}
-analytic universal character
\lambda_{R}\colon T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to R^{\times}
. Let
\kappa_{R}=-w_{M_{G}}^{\operatorname{max}}\cdot(w_{n}\star\lambda_{R})
. This satisfies Assumption
5.3.12
. Furthermore, the specialisation
\kappa_{z}
of
\kappa_{R}
at any dominant classical weight
z\in\operatorname{Spa}(R,R^{+})
satisfies the assumptions in Definition
2.1.3
and
C(\kappa_{z}^{*})^{-}=\{w_{n}\}
. Fix a rational number
h\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
which is larger than
v_{p}(\theta_{\pi,p}(t_{\operatorname{aux}}))
. Note that
\kappa_{z_{0}}=\kappa
and
\lambda_{z_{0}}=\lambda
.

Let
\Omega=\operatorname{Spa}(S,S^{+})\subset\operatorname{Spa}(R,R^{+})
denote open neighbourhood of
z_{0}
as in Theorem
8.6.9
, and let
\Upsilon\subset\Omega(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
denote the subset of classical dominant weights. Let
\eta_{z_{0},1,s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}\in\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{z_{0}}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\operatorname{ss}(\lambda_{z_{0}}))}
denote the unique class corresponding to
\eta^{\operatorname{cl}}_{\pi,1}
via the classicality isomorphisms in Theorem
8.6.5
and Theorem
8.6.9
. It is an eigenvector for the action of
\mathbb{T}^{-}_{L}
with eigencharacter
\theta_{\pi}
. We claim that, after possibly shrinking
\Omega
, we can lift
\eta_{z_{0},1,s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}
to a class

\eta\in\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}.

Indeed, let
I_{\pi}
denote the kernel of
\mathbb{T}^{-}_{L}\xrightarrow{\theta_{\pi}}L
and let
I_{S}
denote the kernel of the composition
\mathbb{T}^{-}_{S}\to\mathbb{T}^{-}_{L}\xrightarrow{\theta_{\pi}}L
, where the first map is induced from specialisation at
z_{0}
. Let
S_{0}
denote the (rigid) localisation of
S
at the maximal ideal corresponding to
z_{0}
. The Tor-spectral sequence:

E_{2}^{i,j}\colon\operatorname{Tor}^{S_{0}}_{-i}\left(\operatorname{H}^{j}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}_{I_{S}},L\right)\Rightarrow\operatorname{H}^{i+j}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{z_{0}}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}_{I_{\pi}}

and the vanishing/classicality results in Theorem
8.6.5
and Theorem
8.6.9
imply that

\operatorname{H}^{j}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}_{I_{S}}=0\quad\text{ for }j\neq n-1

and
\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}_{I_{S}}
is free of finite-rank over
S_{0}
(here we are using the assumption that
\theta_{\pi,p}
is small slope, and the local criterion for flatness). Furthermore, we have

\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}_{I_{S}}\otimes_{S_{0}}L=\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{z_{0}}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}_{I_{\pi}}

and
\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}_{I_{S}}
is a direct factor of
\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}\otimes_{S}S_{0}
. This implies that we can shrink the neighbourhood
\Omega
around
z_{0}
and lift
\eta_{z_{0},1,s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}
to a class
\eta\in\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}
.

Set
\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}=\{z_{0}\}
and note that
\Sigma^{\operatorname{int}}
in Theorem
8.8.3
is equal to
\{z_{0}\}\times\Sigma_{\pi}
. Furthermore, the set
\Sigma
is Zariski dense in
\Omega_{1}
(because
\lambda_{z,n-1,\tau_{0}}-\lambda_{z,n,\tau_{0}}
and
\lambda_{z,n,\tau}
become arbitrarily large as
z
runs over
\Upsilon
). We can therefore apply Theorem
8.8.3
and define
\mathscr{L}_{p,\phi}(\pi,-)=\operatorname{sp}_{z_{0}}\Xi(\eta)
, where
\operatorname{sp}_{z_{0}}
denotes the specialisation at
z_{0}\in\Omega
. Let
\nu_{n-1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{sgn}(w_{\operatorname{GL}_{n-1}}^{\operatorname{max}})=(-1)^{(n-1)(n-2)/2}
denote the sign of the longest Weyl element for
\operatorname{GL}_{n-1}
. Then,
\mathscr{L}_{p,\phi}(\pi,-)
has the following interpolation property:

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{p,\phi}(\pi,\hat{\chi})

\displaystyle=(1-p^{-1})\left(\frac{\theta_{\pi,p}(t_{0})}{\theta_{\pi,p}(t_{1})}\right)^{e_{\tau_{0}}}p^{e_{\tau_{0}}\kappa_{n+1,\tau_{0}}}\chi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}_{0}}}(p)^{-e_{\tau_{0}}}\chi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}_{0}}}(-1)\mathscr{G}(\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}_{0}})\operatorname{Ev}_{\kappa,j,\chi,\beta}(\eta^{\operatorname{cl}}_{\pi,\beta})

\displaystyle=(1-p^{-1})\cdot\mathscr{E}_{p}(\pi,\chi)\cdot\chi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}_{0}}}(-\nu_{n-1})\left(\prod_{\tau\neq\tau_{0}}\chi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}}}(-1)^{n}\right)\alpha_{p}^{e}\delta_{B}(t_{p}^{e})\cdot\operatorname{Ev}_{\kappa,j,\chi,\beta}(\eta^{\operatorname{cl}}_{\pi,\beta})

where
\beta\geq\operatorname{max}\{e_{\tau}:\tau\in\Psi\}
and for the second equality we have used
\kappa_{n+1,\tau_{0}}=-1-\lambda_{n,\tau_{0}}
. Set

\mathscr{P}_{\pi,\chi}(\psi)=\int_{[\mathbf{H}]}\psi(h)\chi^{\prime}\left(\frac{\operatorname{det}h_{2}}{\operatorname{det}h_{1}}\right)dh

for any
\psi\in\pi
. Note that this is defines an equivariant linear map
\mathscr{P}_{\pi,\chi}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{A})}(\pi,(\chi^{\prime})^{-1}\circ\nu)
. We now apply Proposition
5.3.18
and see that

\operatorname{Ev}_{\kappa,j,\chi,\beta}(\eta^{\operatorname{cl}}_{\pi,\beta})=(2\pi i)^{-(n-1)}\operatorname{Vol}(K_{H,\beta};dh)\mathscr{P}_{\pi,\chi}(\psi_{\beta}^{[j]}).

Finally, using the transformation properties in Appendix
B
, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{\pi,\chi}(\psi_{\beta}^{[j]})

\displaystyle=\chi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}_{0}}}(-1)^{n-1}\chi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}_{0}}}(\nu_{n-1})(\alpha_{p}^{e}\delta_{B}(t_{p}^{e}))^{-1}\prod_{\tau\in\Psi}p^{(\beta-e_{\tau})n(2n-1)}\chi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}}}(-1)^{n}p^{e_{\tau}n(2n-1)}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{\pi,\chi}(\phi_{e}^{[j]})

\displaystyle=\chi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}_{0}}}(-\nu_{n-1})(\alpha_{p}^{e}\delta_{B}(t_{p}^{e}))^{-1}p^{\beta[F^{+}:\mathbb{Q}]n(2n-1)}\prod_{\tau\neq\tau_{0}}\chi_{\mathfrak{p}_{\bar{\tau}}}(-1)^{n}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{\pi,\chi}(\phi_{e}^{[j]}).

Here we have used the relation between
\hat{\gamma}
and
{{}^{t}u_{\operatorname{sph}}^{-1}}
described in Lemma
2.1.2
. We now define

(\star)=(1-p^{-1})\operatorname{Vol}(K_{H,\beta})p^{\beta[F^{+}:\mathbb{Q}]n(2n-1)}

which is a non-zero rational number which is independent of
\beta
(it only depends on
n
and the volume of
K^{p}\cap\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
). The result follows.
∎

9.3.
Variation in Coleman families

We continue with the notation introduced in the previous sections – in particular, let
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
satisfying the assumptions in §
9.1
(except Remark
9.1.3
). To construct
p
-adic families through
\pi
and a
p
-adic distribution associated with these families, we impose the following additional hypotheses:

•

We assume that the finite primes in
S
split in
E/\mathbb{Q}
.

•

(
\phi
is new away from
p
) We assume that there exists compact open subgroup
K_{S}^{\operatorname{new}}\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f,S})
such that
\phi_{S}\in\pi_{f,S}^{K_{S}^{\operatorname{new}}}
and
\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{f,S}^{K_{S}^{\operatorname{new}}}=1
(note that, under the above assumption on
S
, such a compact open always exists by the local newform theory for general linear groups – see
[
Gra21
, Remark 6.1.2]
). We assume that
K_{S}
is a normal subgroup of
K_{S}^{\operatorname{new}}
. If we set
K^{\operatorname{new}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}K_{S}^{\operatorname{new}}K^{S}
, then we have
\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{f}^{K}=1
.

•

(
\phi_{p}
is a
p
-regular
p
-stabilisation) We assume that the generalised eigenspace of
\pi_{p}^{K^{G}_{\operatorname{Iw}}(p)}
associated with the character
\theta_{\pi,p}
is one-dimensional.

Under these assumptions, we say that
\phi
is a
p
-regular
p
-stabilisation which is new away from
p
. In what follows, the Shimura varieties
S_{\mathbf{G},\operatorname{Iw}}(p^{\beta})
will have prime-to-
p
level given by
K^{p}
. Note that the finite group
K_{S}^{\operatorname{new}}/K_{S}
acts on these Shimura varieties.

Proposition 9.3.1
.

Let
I_{\pi}
denote the kernel of the map
\theta_{\pi}\colon\mathbb{T}_{L}^{-}\to L
. Then

\operatorname{H}^{n-1}\left(\mathcal{S}_{G,\operatorname{Iw}}(p),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right)_{I_{\pi}}^{K_{S}^{\operatorname{new}}/K_{S}}

is one-dimensional over
L
.

Proof.

Note that it is enough to prove the analogous statement over
\mathbb{C}
, i.e., that the
\mathbb{C}
-vector space

\operatorname{H}^{n-1}\left(S(\mathbb{C}),\mathscr{M}_{G,\kappa^{*}}\right)_{I_{\pi}}^{K_{S}^{\operatorname{new}}/K_{S}},\quad\quad S\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}S_{\mathbf{G},\operatorname{Iw}}(p),

is one dimensional (where by abuse of notation,
I_{\pi}
now denotes the kernel of the map
\theta_{\pi}\colon\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{C}}^{-}\to\mathbb{C}
). Suppose that
\sigma
is a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
such that
\sigma_{\infty}
is cohomological. Let
S^{\prime}\supset S
be a finite set of primes including all primes where
\mathbf{G}
and
\sigma
are ramified. Then there exists an automorphic representation
\Sigma
of
\operatorname{GL}_{1}(\mathbb{A}_{E})\times\operatorname{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
such that
\Sigma^{S^{\prime}}\cong\operatorname{BC}^{S^{\prime}}(\sigma^{S^{\prime}})
, where
\operatorname{BC}^{S^{\prime}}
denotes the unramified base-change outside the set of places
S^{\prime}
(see
[
Shi14
]
). If
\Pi
denotes the base-change of
\pi
to an automorphic representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{1}(\mathbb{A}_{E})\times\operatorname{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
, then by strong multiplicity one for automorphic representations of general linear groups (see
[
JS81
, Theorem 4.4]
), if
\pi^{S}\cong\sigma^{S}
then we must have
\Pi\cong\Sigma
. Furthermore, since every prime in
S
splits in
E/\mathbb{Q}
, this implies that
\pi_{f,S}\cong\sigma_{f,S}
(see
[
Shi14
, Theorem A.1(2)]
). Hence the condition
\pi^{S}\cong\sigma^{S}
implies that
\pi_{f}\cong\sigma_{f}
. The proposition now follows from the same proof as in
[
Gra21
, Corollary 6.2.3]
(note that we do not need to assume that
\Pi
is cuspidal).
∎

We now prove the main theorem on the existence of
p
-adic
L
-functions as
\pi
varies in a Coleman family.

Theorem 9.3.2
.

Let
\pi
be as above and let
\mathcal{W}_{0}
denote the
n[F^{+}:\mathbb{Q}]
-dimensional weight space over
\operatorname{Spa}(L,\mathcal{O}_{L})
as in the proof of Theorem
9.2.2
. Then (after possibly increasing
L
by a finite extension) there exists a open affinoid neighbourhood
\Omega=\operatorname{Spa}(\mathscr{O}_{\Omega})\subset\mathcal{W}_{0}
containing
\lambda_{\pi}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lambda
such that:

(1)

There exists a Zariski dense subset
\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}\subset\Omega(L)
of classical
8
8

8

I.e. the weights which are induced from a self-dual dominant algebraic character of the torus
T
.
weights and a morphism
\theta_{\underline{\pi}}\colon\mathbb{T}_{\mathscr{O}_{\Omega}}^{-}\to\mathscr{O}_{\Omega}
such that:

•

For any
z\in\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}
, there exists a unique (up to isomorphism) cuspidal automorphic representation
\underline{\pi}_{z}
satisfying the assumptions in §
9.1
and at the start of §
9.3
(with respect to the same compact open subgroups
K
and
K^{\operatorname{new}}
) such that the specialisation of
\theta_{\underline{\pi}}
at
z
is equal to
\theta_{\underline{\pi}_{z}}
(and the Harish-Chandra parameter of
(\underline{\pi}_{z})_{\infty}
is of the form
w_{n}\cdot(\lambda_{z}+\rho_{G})
, where
\lambda_{z}
is the self-dual dominant character corresponding to
z
).

•

The specialisation of
\theta_{\underline{\pi}}
at
\lambda_{\pi}
is equal to
\theta_{\pi}
.

•

The set
\Sigma_{\underline{\pi}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigcup_{z\in\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}}\{z\}\times\Sigma_{\underline{\pi}_{z}}
is Zariski dense in
\Omega\times\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{N}p^{\infty})
.

(2)

For any
z\in\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}
, let
\mathscr{L}_{p}(\underline{\pi}_{z},-)\in\mathscr{D}^{\operatorname{la}}(\Gal(F_{\mathfrak{N}p^{\infty}}/F),L)
denote the locally analytic distribution in Theorem
9.2.2
associated with a fixed choice of
p
-regular
p
-stabilisation
\phi_{z}
for
\underline{\pi}_{z}
which is new away from
p
. Then there exist constants
\{c_{z}\in L^{\times}:z\in\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}\}
and a locally analytic distribution
\mathscr{L}_{p}(\underline{\pi},-)\in\mathscr{D}^{\operatorname{la}}(\Gal(F_{\mathfrak{N}p^{\infty}}/F),\mathscr{O}_{\Omega})
such that

\operatorname{sp}_{z}\mathscr{L}_{p}(\underline{\pi},-)=c_{z}\cdot\mathscr{L}_{p}(\underline{\pi}_{z},-)

for any
z\in\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}
, where
\operatorname{sp}_{z}
denotes the specialisation at
z
.

Proof.

Following the proof of Theorem
9.2.2
, there exist an open affinoid neighbourhood
\Omega=\operatorname{Spa}(\mathscr{O}_{\Omega})=\operatorname{Spa}(S,S^{+})
of
\lambda_{\pi}
and a cohomology class

\eta\in\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h)}

lifting
\eta_{z_{0},1,s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}
(where
z_{0}
denotes the point corresponding to
\lambda_{\pi}
). By Proposition
9.3.1
, we see that
I_{\pi}
defines a point on the eigenvariety (over
\mathcal{W}_{0}
), and the projection to
\mathcal{W}_{0}
is étale at this point. This implies that (after possibly shrinking
\Omega
), there exists a morphism
\theta_{\underline{\pi}}\colon\mathbb{T}_{S}^{-}\to S
with kernel
I_{\underline{\pi}}
such that

\eta\in\operatorname{H}^{n-1}_{w_{n},s_{0}\operatorname{-an}}(\kappa_{S}^{*};1)^{(-,\dagger,\leq h),K_{S}^{\operatorname{new}}/K_{S}}_{I_{\underline{\pi}}}

is a basis of this one-dimensional free
S
-module. It is also an eigenvector for the action of
\mathbb{T}_{S}^{-}
with eigencharacter
\theta_{\underline{\pi}}
. Let
\Upsilon\subset\Omega(L)
be the subset of classical weights (which is Zariski dense). Then, by the same argument as in
[
Gra21
, Theorem 6.2.5]
and after possibly shrinking
\Omega
, the specialisation of
\theta_{\underline{\pi}}
at
z\in\Upsilon
determines a unique (up to isomorphism) cuspidal automorphic representation
\underline{\pi}_{z}
of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
such that:

•

(\underline{\pi}_{z})_{\infty}\otimes V_{\kappa_{z}}^{*}
has non-vanishing
(\overline{\mathfrak{p}}_{\circ},K^{\circ})
-cohomology in degree
n-1
;

•

\underline{\pi}_{z}
satisfies the assumptions in §
9.1
and §
9.3
, except
(\underline{\pi}_{z})_{\infty}
may not lie in the discrete series.

We let
\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}\subset\Upsilon
denote the subset of classical weights
z
where
(\underline{\pi}_{z})_{\infty}
lies in the discrete series with Harish-Chandra parameter
w_{n}\cdot(\lambda_{z}+\rho_{G})
. By
[
Har90a
, Theorem 3.5 & Lemma 3.6.1]
, we see that one can force this condition by assuming that
\lambda_{z}
is sufficiently regular, therefore
\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}
is still Zariski dense in
\Omega
.

The rest of the theorem now follows by applying the main construction in Theorem
8.8.3
to the class
\eta
, i.e., we define
\mathscr{L}_{p}(\underline{\pi},-)=\Xi(\eta)
. Note that
\eta
does indeed specialise to small slope eigenvectors at points in
\Upsilon^{\operatorname{int}}
. The constants
c_{z}
measure the difference between the choices of
p
-regular
p
-stabilisations for
\underline{\pi}_{z}
which are new away from
p
and the specialisations of the cohomology class
\eta
.
∎

9.4.
The version for non-similitude groups

We finish by explaining how one can deduce the versions of Theorem
9.2.2
and Theorem
9.3.2
for non-similitude unitary groups given in the introduction. Firstly, we note that for any cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}_{0}(\mathbb{A})
, we can lift this to a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
such that
A_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A})
acts trivially (see
[
GS23
, Proposition 3.11]
and the references therein). Here
A_{\mathbf{G}}
denotes the maximal
\mathbb{Q}
-split torus in the centre of
\mathbf{G}
. Conversely, the irreducible constituents of a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
(such that
A_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A})
acts trivially) restricted to
\mathbf{G}_{0}(\mathbb{A})
all lie in the same
L
-packet. We also have extension and restriction results for the local components of these automorphic representations. This means that:

•

We can lift an automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}_{0}(\mathbb{A})
and the test data satisfying the assumptions in §
1.1
(resp. §
1.1
and §
1.1.1
) to an automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
and test data satisfying the assumptions in §
9.1
(resp. §
9.1
and §
9.3
), with the additional property that
A_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A})
acts trivially.

•

For any automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
such that
A_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A})
acts trivially and test data satisfying the assumptions in §
9.1
(resp. §
9.1
and §
9.3
), we can find an irreducible constituent of its restriction to
\mathbf{G}_{0}(\mathbb{A})
and test data satisfying the assumptions in §
1.1
(resp. §
1.1
and §
1.1.1
).

We can now deduce Theorem
A
and Theorem
B
from Theorem
9.2.2
and Theorem
9.3.2
respectively. Indeed, we can force the additional condition that
A_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A})
acts trivially for the family
\underline{\pi}
by the same argument as in
[
Gra21
, Corollary 8.2.4]
, and we can relate the automorphic periods by using the fact that
[\mathbf{H}]/A_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A})
is a disjoint union of finitely many translates of
[\mathbf{H}_{0}]
(see
[
Gra21
, Remark 8.2.8]
).

Appendix A
Some representation theory

In this appendix, we record some results from the representation theory of general linear groups which are used in §
5.3.3
. The notation in the first part of this appendix (up until §
A.1
) differs from the rest of the article.

Let
1\leq a\leq b
be integers, and set
G=\operatorname{GL}_{a+b}
. We let
H\subset G
denote the subgroup
H=\operatorname{GL}_{a}\times\operatorname{GL}_{b}
embedded block diagonally. We consider
G
and
H
as algebraic groups over
\mathbb{Q}
(or any field of characteristic zero). We let
T\subset H\subset G
denote the standard diagonal torus.

Notation A.0.1
.

Let
R=\mathbb{Q}[T_{1},\dots,T_{a}]/(T_{1}^{2},\dots,T_{a}^{2})
and let
\mathfrak{m}_{R}\subset R
denote the maximal ideal. Let
t=\operatorname{diag}(t_{1},\dots,t_{a})\in M_{a\times a}(R)
denote a diagonal matrix with
t_{i}\in\mathfrak{m}_{R}
for all
i=1,\dots,a
, where
M_{a\times a}(R)
denotes the space of
(a\times a)
-matrices with coefficients in
R
. For any permutation
\sigma\in S_{a}
(which we view as a permutation of the set
\{1,\dots,a\}
), we set

\displaystyle Y^{(a)}_{\sigma}(t)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Y^{(a)}_{\sigma}(t_{1},\dots,t_{a})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left(\delta_{\sigma(i),j}t_{i}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq a}\in M_{a\times a}(R)

\displaystyle X^{(a)}_{\sigma}(t)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}X^{(a)}_{\sigma}(t_{1},\dots,t_{a})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{I}_{a\times a}+Y^{(a)}_{\sigma}(t)\in\operatorname{GL}_{a}(R)

where
\operatorname{I}_{a\times a}
is the
(a\times a)
identity matrix, and
\delta_{-,-}
denotes the Kronecker delta function.

We have the following useful lemma:

Lemma A.0.2
.

Let
t=\operatorname{diag}(t_{1},\dots,t_{a})
and
\sigma
be as in Notation
A.0.1
. Let

\sigma=\sigma_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\sigma_{r}

denote the decomposition of
\sigma
into cycles, and let
|\sigma_{i}|\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{k\in\{1,\dots,a\}:\sigma_{i}(k)\neq k\}
. Set
m_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{min}(\sigma_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{min}|\sigma_{i}|
. Then:

(1)

X^{(a)}_{\sigma}(t)
has an Iwahori decomposition

X^{(a)}_{\sigma}(t)=X^{(a)}_{\sigma}(t)^{+}\cdot X^{(a)}_{\sigma}(t)^{-}

where
X^{(a)}_{\sigma}(t)^{+}
(resp.
X^{(a)}_{\sigma}(t)^{-}
) lies in the standard upper-triangular Borel subgroup (resp. lower-triangular unipotent subgroup) of
\operatorname{GL}_{a}(R)
.

(2)

The projection of
X^{(a)}_{\sigma}(t)^{+}
to the standard diagonal torus is of the form
\operatorname{diag}(x_{1},\dots,x_{a})
with

x_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\text{ if }j\neq m_{i}\text{ for any }i=1,\dots,r\\
\left(1+\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma_{i})\prod_{k\in|\sigma_{i}|}t_{k}\right)&\text{ if }j=m_{i}\end{array}\right.

where
\operatorname{sgn}(-)
denotes the sign of a permutation.

Proof.

Part (1) is immediate because
t
has coefficients in
\mathfrak{m}_{R}
and
R
is an Artinian local ring. For part (2), we will prove this by induction on
a
. Clearly the claim is true when
a=1
, so we suppose
a>1
.

Suppose that
\sigma(a)=a
. Then

X^{(a)}_{\sigma}(t_{1},\dots,t_{a})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}X_{\sigma}^{(a-1)}(t_{1},\dots,t_{a-1})&\\
&1+t_{a}\end{array}\right)

where the right-hand side is a block matrix, with top left block of size
(a-1)\times(a-1)
. The claim now follows from the induction hypothesis.

Finally, suppose that
\sigma(a)\neq a
(so in particular,
\sigma(a)<a
). Define a new permutation
\tau\in S_{a-1}
by removing
a
from its (non-trivial) cycle, i.e. we define

\tau(j)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\sigma(a)&\text{ if }j=\sigma^{-1}(a)\\
\sigma(j)&\text{ if }j\neq\sigma^{-1}(a)\end{array}\right.

for
j=1,\dots,a-1
. Suppose, without loss of generality, that
a\in|\sigma_{1}|
. Then the decomposition of
\tau
into cycles is of the form
\tau=\tau_{1}\circ\sigma_{2}\circ\cdots\circ\sigma_{r}
, where
\tau_{1}
is the cycle obtained by omitting
a
. Note that
\operatorname{sgn}(\tau_{1})=-\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma_{1})
. For
1<i\leq r
, set
\tau_{i}=\sigma_{i}
.

Let
Z
denote the
(a\times a)
matrix which is zero everywhere, except in the
(a,\sigma(a))
-entry where it is equal to
-t_{a}
. Then

X_{\sigma}^{(a)}(t_{1},\dots,t_{a})\cdot(\operatorname{I}_{a\times a}+Z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}U&V\\
&1\end{array}\right)

where the right-hand side is a block matrix with
U\in\operatorname{GL}_{a-1}(R)
, and the matrix
U
is given by

U=X_{\tau}^{(a-1)}(t_{1},\dots,t_{\sigma^{-1}(a)-1},-t_{\sigma^{-1}(a)}t_{a},t_{\sigma^{-1}(a)+1},\dots,t_{a-1}).

Using the induction hypothesis, we therefore see that the projection of
X^{(a)}_{\sigma}(t)^{+}
to the standard diagonal torus is of the form
\operatorname{diag}(x_{1},\dots,x_{a-1},1)
with

x_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\text{ if }j\neq\operatorname{min}(\tau_{i})\;(=\operatorname{min}(\sigma_{i}))\text{ for any }i=1,\dots,r\\
\left(1+\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma_{i})\prod_{k\in|\sigma_{i}|}t_{k}\right)&\text{ if }j=\operatorname{min}(\sigma_{i})\text{ with }i>1\\
\left(1+\operatorname{sgn}(\tau_{1})\cdot(-t_{\sigma^{-1}(a)}t_{a})\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in|\tau_{1}|\\
k\neq\sigma^{-1}(a)\end{subarray}}t_{k}\right)&\text{ if }j=\operatorname{min}(\sigma_{1})\end{array}\right.

The claim now follows from
\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma_{1})=-\operatorname{sgn}(\tau_{1})
and
|\tau_{1}|\cup\{a\}=|\sigma_{1}|
.
∎

The above lemma will be used in the proof of the following proposition. Let
\mathbb{Q}[G]
denote the ring of algebraic functions on
G
. This comes equipped with an action of
G
given by
(g\cdot f)(-)=f(g^{-1}-)
(for
g\in G
and
f\in\mathbb{Q}[G]
), and we can differentiate this to obtain an action of
\mathfrak{g}=\operatorname{Lie}(G)
. We will denote this action of
\mathfrak{g}
by
\star
. We denote by

u\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&u^{\prime}\\
&1\end{array}\right)\in G(\mathbb{Q})

the block matrix (with top left block of size
(a\times a)
, and bottom right of size
(b\times b)
), where
u^{\prime}
is the
(a\times b)
-matrix given by

(u^{\prime})_{i,j}=\delta_{b+1-i,j},\quad\quad i\in\{1,\dots,a\},j\in\{1,\dots,b\}.

We let
w_{a}\in\operatorname{GL}_{a}(\mathbb{Q})
denote the antidiagonal matrix with each non-zero entry equal to
1
.

Proposition A.0.3
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{Q}[G]
be an algebraic function such that

•

f(h^{-1}-)=(\operatorname{det}h_{1})^{-\nu_{1}}(\operatorname{det}h_{2})^{-\nu_{2}}f(-)
for all
h=(h_{1},h_{2})\in\operatorname{GL}_{a}\times\operatorname{GL}_{b}=H

•

f(-b)=\kappa(b^{-1})f(-)
for all
b
in the standard lower-triangular Borel subgroup of
G

for some integers
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
and algebraic character
\kappa=(\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{a+b})\in X^{*}(T)
(inflated to the lower-triangular Borel subgroup). Let
\sigma\colon\{1,\dots,a\}\hookrightarrow\{1,\dots,b\}
be an injective map, and set

\mu_{\sigma}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\prod_{i=1}^{a}E_{i,a+b+1-\sigma(i)}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})

where
E_{i,j}
denotes the elementary matrix with non-zero entry in the
(i,j)
-th place (and the product takes place in
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
).

(1)

If
\operatorname{im}(\sigma)\cap\{a+1,\dots,b\}\neq\varnothing
then

(\mu_{\sigma}\star f)(u)=0.

(2)

Suppose
\operatorname{im}(\sigma)\cap\{a+1,\dots,b\}=\varnothing
(so
\sigma
is a permutation of
\{1,\dots,a\}
). Set
\operatorname{max}(\sigma_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{max}|\sigma_{i}|
, where
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\sigma_{r}
is the decomposition of
\sigma
into cycles. Then

(\mu_{\sigma}\star f)(u)=(-1)^{a}\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{r}(\nu_{1}+\kappa_{\operatorname{max}(\sigma_{i})})\cdot f(u).

Proof.

Unless specified otherwise, in this proof any
(a+b)\times(a+b)
matrix written as
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}*&*\\
*&*\end{smallmatrix}\right)
means that matrix is written as a block matrix with top left block of size
(a\times a)
(and hence the bottom right block is of size
(b\times b)
). We continue to denote
R=\mathbb{Q}[T_{1},\dots,T_{a}]/(T_{1}^{2},\dots,T_{a}^{2})
.

Let
C\in M_{a\times b}(R)
, and let
C^{\prime}\in M_{a\times a}(R)
denote the matrix
(C^{\prime})_{i,j}=C_{i,b-a+j}
(i.e. the right-hand
(a\times a)
block of
C
). Suppose that
C^{\prime}\in\operatorname{GL}_{a}(R)
. Then there exists a matrix
Z\in\operatorname{GL}_{b}(R)
in the standard lower-triangular unipotent such that
CZ\in M_{a\times b}(R)
satisfies

(CZ)_{i,j}=0\text{ if }j\in\{1,\dots,b-a\},\text{ and }(CZ)_{i,j}=(C^{\prime})_{i,j-(b-a)}\text{ if }j\in\{b-a+1,\dots,b\}.

We now prove part (1). Let
l=\sigma(n)\in\{a+1,\dots,b\}
. Let
U_{\sigma}\in M_{a\times b}(R)
denote the matrix

(U_{\sigma})_{i,j}=\delta_{b+1-\sigma(i),j}T_{i}.

Then

(A.0.4)

(\mu_{\sigma}\star f)(u)=(-1)^{a}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial T_{1}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial T_{a}}f\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&U_{\sigma}\\
&1\end{array}\right)\cdot u\right)\right|_{T_{1}=0,\dots,T_{a}=0}

where one is also permitted to permute the order of differentiation freely. The power of
(-1)
arises because
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&U_{\sigma}\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-U_{\sigma}\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. We claim that
f\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&U_{\sigma}\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\cdot u\right)
doesn’t depend on
T_{n}
(where
n
is the element such that
\sigma(n)=l
), which will give the claim because it will be killed by
\frac{\partial}{\partial T_{n}}
. We have

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&U_{\sigma}\\
&1\end{array}\right)\cdot u=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&U_{\sigma}+u^{\prime}\\
&1\end{array}\right).

Let
C=U_{\sigma}+u^{\prime}
. Then the right-hand
(a\times a)
block is invertible, so by the above argument we can find a lower triangular unipotent matrix
Z\in\operatorname{GL}_{b}(R)
such that
(CZ)_{i,j}=0
for
j\in\{1,\dots,b-a\}
, and
(CZ)_{i,j}
doesn’t depend on
T_{n}
if
j\in\{b-a+1,\dots,b\}
